LADIES' LETTER.

Mr Masa NELLIE, The new lace straw, so thick and coarse in part, and so gossamer, is an excellent choice tor a spring hat, and the turban shape a good one to desire. Trimmed with a scarf of tulle, flecked with chenille and airy rosettes, it is spring-like, yet not too much so. Picture hats are adorned at the back with large flat bows, and in front have a long buckle, threaded through the band round the crown. A hat itself may be a fanciful construction, perhaps of pleated gauze on a chenille frame the gauze a substantial variant of the usual fArie.

For silk blouses, glace silk still holds its own, although the softer varieties are rivalling it seriously. Some of the neatest glace blouses are tucked all over with the exception of the deep collar of the sailor type, which is more often stitched. The small pointec, or spade-shaped vest, together with the under-sleeves, are of white silk or mousseline, and a pretty ribbon tie of one kind )r another generally completes the blouse. Blouses of Japanese silk intended for wear a little latez on, when warm days may be reasonably expected, are Icquently made without a neck-band, the small yoke of lace or tucked mousseline being cut slightly square at the base of the throat. This yoke can be lined or not, and the under-sleeves, if there are such, must correspond with it. Braces of lace-insertion are seen on many blouses of this sort, but, on blouses of Paisley or bandana silk, bretelles of satin or velvet, ribbon look better than lace elms.

F ~;:.

C.-

saw a nice flower hat with a bow and ends of black velvet at the back. Such a hat took the form of a sweeping up-turned coal-scuttle brim, all palest rose pink gauze, of great height, decorated with the proverbial wreaths of roses at the base of the brim upon the coiffure, and snugly arranged at fhe back to meet a knot of brightly burnished hair, sad more roses. Delicious effects are gained by scaled tulle, in other words by tulle so arranged that the scales of a big fish are suggested. It is carried out over a shimmering foundation of gold sr silver tissue, with the happiest results. A very tnrious but lovely chartreuse, the colour of a sar- peot's skin, was thus manipulated over glistening ;old tissue in the beautiful example mentioned. Ciao shape is the turban, with a dull gold buckle &aping the brim, and bronze green ostrich plumes starting from it, flecked with a few striking white fronds.

In a pretty frock of pink cloth the skirt is after 4111111 of the new designs which simulate & treble dtirt. It is needless to say that there are not in reality three skirts running the whole way up to the waist. This would make a thickness some- thing like that displayed by the fishwomen of *toted resort whose wealth is shown by the mum- bar of petticoats they wear. The form of the upper part of the skirt is carried up princess fashion to the height of the ordinary corselet or shaped belt. Thss is a fashion that is very becoming to most figures. In one instance the bodice is finished elith a triple bolero, simulated just as the skirt is. The trimming with which both skirts and holerc are ed4erl is a black fancy braid. The collar is Week silk covered with lace, which may be either Meek or a deep string colour.. This always looks very well with pink, especially when black plays any part in the composition. The sleeves carry out the triple idea, and are of the modified pagoda or bell shape, over cuffs of white muslin and in. section to match the vest. The black velvet tie has ornamented ends, after the present style. A band of lace covers the collar.

Appearances show that this will he a season fot eat-door amusements and simp!icity, rather th.it otherwise. The patterns and colourings in thesP batiste, cotton and muslins are more charniing steer than ever before; the pinks are so rosy, and the blues as cerulean as a midsummer sky, whilst the flowery sprays and garlands suggest treasuret from an old•workl garden. The majority of these Crocks Shoukl be sent to the cleaners, rather than ito the laundry, but if they are to be washed, both bodice and skirt should be free from any thWag or foundation, and with as few folds or gathers as possible. Alpacas and mohairs will be fashionable for morning gowns for street wear this season, treated with a good deal of coarse lace as trimming. The pin-spot alpacas are effective and stylish-looking, and make up well with fronts and revers of satin the same shade as the spot. In fact, the alpaca gown is being made to look very dressy, for the 'skirt and bodice are treated with open herring - boning, in coarse, twisted silk, which reveals a different-coloured silk lining underneath. The open work or herring-bone insertion is one of the Leading fancies, and is especially noticeable on eactl silk gowns. Royal blue is a fashionable • colour in glaciS silk just now, and a good model was made with a much-flounced skirt, ,the floulice, continued right tap to the waist, each ffonlice pipeti and corded with black satin and lined with deer cream satin. The bodice had a short bolero 01 black satin, tucked all over, showing the blue glace under-bodice pouched at the waist over a black satin waistband, and opening over an billet vest of deep ecru lace, gathered into a big bow it the centre. This rich Royal blue has a certaii chic of its OPT.

Your loving JENNIE.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The hapos which were entertained that the L-inclun season would revive aft's' th. period of gloom, seem destined to be disappointed. A NEW RAILWAY, PROJECT, • Warrington Affected,

A railway is pr-jected to open out a shorter Ind quicker route Isom Leicester. and districts south thereof on the Great Central Railway to Manchester, Liverpool, Wigan. St Helens, and Southport, and vice versa, and diminiph the distance +and time I etween them; to con- nect tee to ens of Leicester, Derby. Astthourne, Leek, Macclesfield. Manchester (London Road and Central), Pr(stblify, Wilmslow, Warrington (Central), and Li+er- pool (Central), and to open out new districts ind de% e'on their resources.

Commencing by a junction with the Great Central Railway at Be!grave (near .Leicester), the line—abont 74 miles in length—would pass ithrough Thucaston, Wosdhou+e. Sheepshed, Donnington Castle, Blown°, Derby, KOK Langley, Berillsford, Act bourne, Oatrotter, Blore, Cau'don, Leek, Meerbrooke, Winele, High Button. Macclesfield, Prestbury, Wilmslow, and Stye', and terminate by a junction with the Great Central Railway (Cheshire Lines) at Baguley. The line would open nut a direct route and give improved railway cern- , munication between Leicester, &c. and Manchester, Liverpool. Southport and Wigan and effect a saving of about twenty miles between Leicester and Manches- ter, and thirty miles hctween Leicester and Liverpool as compared with that via Ntiogbam, Sheffield. and Pent. stone, and be about ten miles shorter than the Midland 11 route, with a corresponding sawn gif time. It would. I moreover. intimately connect and give additional facili- ties to the towns of Leicester, Derby, A‘hbourne. Leek, Macclesfield, &c., and also for the conveyance of farm and other produce to the neighbouring markets. The connection which by this proposed railway should be effected between the important systems of the Great Central Railway and the Cheshire Lin.s would ensure a considerable saving of mileage and time, and greatly increase railway accommodatinn to the general public, as it would form an important link in a direct route and bring Leicester and districts south thereof into closer connection with Menehester. Liverpool, and South Lan- cashire. The whole district served by Ma intended railway would thus be placed in direct communication with Macclesfield, Manchester. Warrington, Liverpool, cn the one hand of Leicester and the whole of the Great Central Railway south thereof and London (fdarylebone) on the other. The aavaritages of good railway accommodation ti landowners, residents, farmers, and traders are now en universally admitted that it is almost unnecessary te refer to them. The proposed "Leicester, Derby, Mac- clesfield, Manchester, and Liverpool Junction Railway" would form an important link in the railway system and pass through and open up new districts, and enable the I Great Central Railway Company to extend their system I and shorten their route from Leicester (via Derby, Ash- I bourne. Leek, and Maccleafield) to Manchester, Live,- pool, Southpoit, and South Lancashire generally, and give increased and improved railway communication and accommodation locally and generally:

ANOTHER KNUTSFORD SENSATION.

Married on Thursday Dead on Sunday. Au unique event was witnessed at the KontsfA.d Parish Church on Thursday last week, when Mr T Stephen Jones, a well-known and respected official in the clerical department of his Maj sty'a gaol at Knutsford. eon- j ducted two daughters t the altar and there gave them I away in marriage. The young ladies' names were Eleanor Serv•t and Harriet Ernistine, and they were thd eldest, and second daughters respectively of Mr Jones. VIE TUMORS OF DEATH. The morning of the execution broke dull and ominous. Heavy clouds overhung Henlea, and a cold, drizzling rain made the position of the crowd of morbid sightseers who had come forth to watch the unfurling of the black flag one of extreme discomfort. As the hour of eight approached the watchers increased in ' number, and at length when the strokes or a neighbouring clock commenced to clang, every tongue was stopped and every' eye was riveted upon the raised stonework of the high wall and the solitary flagstaff that flanked it. Every instant the daik messenger of the legal tragedy enacted within those time-marked walls was expected, but the last notes of the clock died away and still came no sign. A minute past the hour, two, three, five, ten, yet no signal. What could have happened? The silence was broken now. Tongues were let loose in eager questioning and self-satisfied explanation. ' The quarter chimed, then the, half-hour. At that moment a small gate opened at the oba of the . wall and two men emerged. They took to their heels and ran madly down the street. They were \jou'inalists, and they made direct for the telegraph- . effice, each man striving to be the first-to despatch the strange news of which he was possessed. , • In London the Evening Herald sub-editor, tired of waiting, had already _sent an extra early morn- ing edition on to the streets with a line announcing the execution of the Fenwick murderer, and WAS wondering why the confirmatory wire he expected had not got through, when the tape-machine started clicking and a message began to reel in. A boy snicked off the first few inches with a pair of scissors, 'pasted the strips on a piece of "copy" paper and 'handed, it to the sub. The man glanced at it, then his jaw dropped and a look of horror came into his eyes. "Good Lord !" he said. "Why, they haven't hanged the man. And that special on the streets!" Ile-pored excitedly over the strips the boy was handing up, and within a few minutes a second edition was in the hands of eager boys and men who shouted aloud their exciting intelligence. The report was this: ~ . "Fenwick Murder. "Extraordinary Scene at the Gallows. "The Drop-bolt Refuses to Act, "Prisoner Yet Unhanged. "The execution of the prisoner, Martin Fenwick, was td have taken place this morning at Henlea, but at the last moment, when the prisoner was blindfolded and strapped, the bolt refused to act. The prisoner was released for a few minutes whilst carpenters set to work to remedy the defect in the machinery, and once more an attempt was made to carry out the last penalty of the law.. But again did the bolt refuse to answer. although Robertson. Lne hangman, flau tested it only a moment before and seemed satisfied with the alteration. „ . _ _

"At this second failure the .C-o-Qem-n-e;d man, who lasd preserved a calm demeanour, lost com;

control of himself. The excitement of thus facins death twiae within a few minutes proved too mug! for him and he fell in a comatose condition upon the scaffolding., At first it was thought that his heart had failed and ..that the shock had proved fatal, but eventually the respiratory exercises of the Medical man in atttendance proved effectual. "The sheriff, however, at this stage undertook the responsitiility of postponing the execution, andi to the relitfl,o, everybody, the prieoner, looking deathly pale, was carried bac-I to his cell, and the horrible proceedings were at an end foil This comprised the first message, but during the day the papers gradually filled up with this latest sensation of the hour. Why ,the drop had faiiod to act nobody knew., It' had 'seemed all right when Robertson had made ready. The small amount of rain that had fallen in the night could have made little difference to the structure. The whole country was full of speculation, and superstitious people were inclined to regard the failure of tho drop as a signal proof of the condemned inan's innocence. The matter had become a public controversy by the next day, and the papers were crowded with letters on the topic, advocates of the abolition of capital punishment making the, terrors Martin had suffered a text upon which to hang their arguments. • In the midst of the, controversy, the one Secretary's order for the fulfilment of the sentence in a week's time was issued.

By the time the day arrived when the man, who had twice., tastedlhe horrors of facing death was once more,to _suffer the agony, public feeling was at very high tension. A public appeal had been issued and signed, pointing out that the prisoner had already uffered as much mental torture as if the dread .penalty had in reality bee a paid. But. the petition fell upon unheeding ears. Upon the morning 'appointed, -.an array of journalists awaited the execution at Henlea Gaol. Three only were admitted. All the dread pre- liminaries were proceeded with. Then came the • critical moment. Robertson pressed the lever. Nothing transpired. He jerked it again. Duce more it failed, and with the perspiration standing thick upon his forehead he removed the prisoner and again tested the machine. . To his amazement 'the trap acted and a yawning pitivits at his feet. "Replace the prisoner," said the governor. But Robertson was now possessed of a belief that some °waist Power had intervened to save the prisoner from hanging. "Not for every pound of money in the kingdom I* he exclaimed. wash my hands of this job." His'face was pale and his soul was - filled with dread.

I

"I shall resign, "• he muttered, as once spin the plisonees hood was removed and Martin, colour- less, with hair turned grey- by the terrible ordeal, was led back to his cell. Never hall such an extraordinary event occurred. Excitement ovet the matter raged from Cornwall to Sutherland, and almost every paper in the land clamoured for the remission of the penalty. A committee visited the gaol the following day and tested the drop:. Twenty times did a com- mitteeman pull the lever and twenty times was the bolt which affected the trap withdrawn and the pit yawned beneath. "Unearthly strange," said one man. "Let me try." He seised., the lever and gave it a jerk. There was no response. An aped engineer who was of the company promptly laid bare the mechanism and revealed the secret. It *was simplicity itself. The end of a loosely-driven nail projected over the nut that had to be released and deterred it from acting. The lever was loose, and it was only when pulled with strain in a certain direction' such as 'would naturally be adopted by the hangman stepping quickly back after adjusting the prisoner, that the nail thus interrupted the ordinary working of the lever. Robertson had never detected this. The lever had acted scores and scores of times during the testing. He had not thought that a slight bearing towards him of the handle whilst he - depressed it would' make such a mighty difference. Stronger than ever raged. the controversy in the, press, and a determined set was made to secure the condemned man's reprieve. In vain did one or - two journals point out that the law was not to be checked in so serious a matter by a mere nail that had worked askew. It was pointed out in the prisoner's interest that he had suffered all tbi agonies of the extreme ji6nalty and that nothing was to be gained by observing the technical letter . of the law. And one morning the warder opened Martin's cell door, bearing a paper in his hand. He had been reprieved. The grey-haired, hollow-eyed, stricken, pre— maturely-aged man who heard this message a pardon fell on his knees and poured out &great cry of thankegiving to God. (lb be continued.)

THE LADDER OF GUILT.

BY lILYSS ROGERS, '

Author of "For Love and the Queen.* "Wedied in Mystery," Ice.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: Douglas Low, madly in love with Grace Hendon, daughter of a neighbouring farmer, attempts to murder his cousin, Martin Fenwick. His aim, however, un- accountably fails, alld Squire Fenwick, his uncle, is the victim instead.

CHAPTER 111. TIIE ARREST. Low dropped his gun an stared in undisguised terror at the face. As he did so the reeds and willows parted, and the owner of the evircounten- ance, which still leered at him, came forward—an old hag, with weather-tanned, wrinkled parchment skin, high cheek-bones, and malice written in every line of her crinkled featuras. Suddenly the-evil vanished from her face. "Thank God for this," she croaked. "There's a judgment on the Fenwicks at last, at last; Jezebel's old eyes have seen it:: Thank God." ; "Hush, you blasphemous old hag Stand out of my way, or—" He checked himself. "It was an accident, I tell you—an accident. Out of my way, woman!" • He scarcely knew what he was saying, but he heard the splash of the pole as Martin, with the dead body of his uncle in the boat, punted across in the direction whence he supposed the shot to have been fired. Low, in his eacitement and terror, had utterly lost all control -of himself, otherwise he would have seen that if he were to plead the event an accident, standing his ground was the Wisest and safest course open to him. The woman laughed again, a low, hollow, cynical, demoniacal laugh. Then she said: "No, it was no accident. He "—she pointed, through the reeds—"he did it. I saw. I hate 'en. It vrill be worth my while?" 'Yes," he said, hurriedly, divining her meaning. "But I must get away from this." "Then go," she said, "I Will stay. The Lord has given you into my hand, you and your hated house. He—the man who lies dead—has lived long under my curse. My husband died through his father—he it was who turned me out of the cottage, so that my boy was born in the wood yonder, born a cripple, and, that other, the man with 'en, I caught 'en thrashing my boy only the other day., Oh, for a man's strength, and I would have struck 'en dead to the earth, I would. I hate 'en, I hate the dead old man, and I hate you. But you can help me to my vengeance. You go. But come again and see me, at the hut ii the woods, and I will tell you what are the tgrms for saving your life. Go now." The woman had spoken in a subdued, though fiercely angry tone, her dark eyes flashing as the words came rapidly from between her toothless gums. Her* malice against the Fenwicks was the outcome of an old feud. Possibly an injustice had been done by the old squire's brother, who had managed the estate for some years. Parr, a loafing, lazy man, had committed numerous 'depredations and was a menace to the neighbourhood. In the hope of clearing the locality of his undNirable presence, the late squire, Robert Fenwick, had Written home from Algiers giving sanction to his brother Edwin to eject the family, a proceeding that was legally warranted by the fact that for three or four years the Parrs had steadfastlr shirked the payment of .rent- Edwin was Away from Fenwick at the time, and the order had been somewhat ruthlessly put into effect by the bailiffs. Two days later the only off- spring of the Farr alliance saw light in the hastily erected lean-to which Parr had fashioned in the wood.

Edwin Fenwick's kind heart was troubled when he heard of the ruthless ejectment, but all repara- tion beyond the liberty to retain the hut in the woods was savagely declined by the Parrs, and when at last Parr, senior, cut short a term of im- prisonment for the manslaughter of a gamekeeper by dying in gaol, he left a heritage of hate and revenge to his wife and his boy. Ezekiel Parr had wrapped himself tightly in this mantle bequeathed by his father, and his boyhood had been spent in waging war against the Chase, and all things human or otherwise appertaining thereto. The squire and Martin had often allowed their pity to intervene between flagrant offence and ' its rightful punishment, but when Martin caught the hulking rebel offering an insult to Grace Hendon, he promptly seized him and soundly thrashed him. The air rang with his cowardly yells for mercy, and the vengeful shrieks of his mother. • This was the incident to which the hag alluded. Jezebel wntched Low glide through the willows. Then she turned to the water. Martin Fenwick was on. the point of landing. The moment he caught sight of her, he had no further doubt in his own mind as to the origin of the mischief. Ezekiel Parr was at the bottom of it. "Woman, who fired that gun? Where is he ? " You ask that ? Do you ? You—you know who fired it;" she continued, stretching her bony fingers towards him. "You fired it—you killed, the old man—and I—saw— you—" •, • "You lie, you wretched old hag. You lie. Somebody fired from the bank." • "I saw you fire," retorted the old woman; "with these eyes I did. The old man's back was turned and you fired at him. You killed 'en." “No, not I," replied Fenwick, "not I. But I 'think I know who did. That scapegrace son of -yours. Where ii he ? Speak, woman, deliver him up or it will be the worse for you," At the mention of her son and the possible 'clanger to-him, old Jezebel shrieked in wild ferror, but she recovered herself •on the instant. For her son was safe. An extraordinary Providence, as she accounted it in this instance, had lodged him in gaol for petty thieving only the night before. He had frequently threatened in his village boasting to be even with the old squire some day, and t pie;on might have rested upon him haterribleb be been u n gprsowling round with gun or dog in search of a breakfast, no unusual practice of his. yt hoeu, young ten:: man's face. bionutslyyoui.n p I will swear to it." Jezebel Parr laughed,not hev,ic swear‘H'Ne brushed it. he east her, and hurried onwards through the wood to the main road seeking heir and the murderer of his uncle. On the far aide of the enclosure he came upon a group of hir labourers on their way to their morning work. They hurried back with him, improvised a stretcher from the bottom board of the punt and slowly bore the body of the old man homewards. The ill news travelled ahead of them. As the melancholy procession passed the lodge gates, the servants, with horror-stricken faces, came nervously and fearfully down from the house to meet it. And in their midst, brushing his way through them. pressed Douglas .Low, hurriedly attired in his dressing-gown. With consummate skill, the guilty actor played his part. Upon whoinsoevei' suspicion of foul play might rest, nobody would believe that the man with drawn, distressed features and bloodshot eyes, who had just hastened from the bedroom, had any hand in the morning's tragedy. "Who has done this? " he cried. "What man is responsible for this terrible accident ?" "It is no accident, Douglas," replied his cousin. "It is murder—deliLerate murder." "Murder!" yhe word trembled upon Low's lips. Did Fenwick suspect ? Did lie know ? A. ghastly pallor overspread his face. His cousin noticed it but did not wonder. The horror of the thing inuat have appealed to every- body. "Yes, somebody fired from the bank. He had his back to ,net the time, and I was looking: over his shoulder for a bird I had dropped. The bullet came from behiSci. I felt the whiz of it at-it skimmed past me. An eighth of an inch nearerlind I inu:t.have been the victim and not he. Poorjold fellow, he never spoke. Then I turned right round and made for the bank, and whom do you think I found ? " - "Whom?" " "That wretched Parr' woman. It was clear to me then who had ramie the mischief. Her son. He had bolted." "You found him ? " gasped Low. Though there was the dantr,er that, to avert suspicion from her son, the gipsy would tell the truth as she knew it, yet he felt that the detection of Ezekiel Parr on the .spot would be quite sufficient to entirely discredit 'what would appear an utterly' improbable: story. The very vehemence of her accusation of Low would tighten the noose around her son's neck. "No, we have not found him yet," said Fenwick. "I hadn't time to make the search myself, but I despatched some of the men. If they are smart they will unearth him. lam positive he is not far off."

The Palace, Liverpool, Bth April, 1911.. Hear 'dr Hindley : —" Since our interview on Saturday, I have thought carefully over the subject of the seat donations in sq. Peter a Chureh I understand that they are only a temporary expedient for a temporary need. but I do not think that you will be justified in abolishing them unti those members of the congreg don to wham they are ob jeetionable are willing to guarantee the church Wardens the amount they bring in. A congregation must be just before It is generous. The ordinary offertories do not bring In a sufficient sum to meet the eurrent expesses, and to aug- ment the very moderate income of your Vicar. Some such means must be used to raise the money. I will also venture to add that In my opinion it is advisable and reasonable to meet the wishes of the old tried and everous members of the congregations, and to appropriate seats to them, providing of course that a considerable part of the church be free and open. When the necessity for sett donations has passed away, seats can still be appropriated without any subscription being given for their use. In such matters the churchwardens have f 01l control and they are bound to take into considera- Mors the wishes of all sections of the congregation. The system which has worked best in my own experience is that In which a church was absolutely free, but IL which the seats were appropriated on one aide of the centre aisle, and unappropriated on the other. With every good wish, believe me ever yours faithfully 10. Improvements at Lymm Station. Important alterations on an extensive scale are being carried out at Lymm railway station, which, when completed, will prove very useful. The villagers have long agitated for various improve- ments, and doubtless what is now being done will give every satisfaction. Each platform is being lengthened by about 50 yards in the direction of Warrington. On the down side the platform roof ,is being extended by,a lolty structure which will afford plenty of light. The opposite platform which up to the present has been very open, is being roofed in and an additional waiting-room '(as well as a boiling office) is being built. The. ,work is proceeding very satisfactorily. The signal box is to be removed to the opposite side. Some time ago the Urban Council" pressed for a bridge to be con- structed over the crossing so as to render it less dangerous to the public,but the company cannot yet see their way to comply with the request, and they do not prop Jae to make a subway, on account of t the main sewer obstructing" the passage. across. Similar idteratiOn are being carried out at Heatley Station.

„ --- OPPORTUNE AND 'URGENT IN THEIR OWN TOWN just now. lie was credibly informed that Sunday trad- ing was becoming very coin non in Warrington, and that more shops wet.: np-n on Sundays now than ever before. _ THE LEADING THOROUGHFARES ON SUNDAY EVENINGS were thronged by hundreds of persons, chiefly young, woo deemed Sunday night the beat time in the week for promenade, and sometimes for boisterous frolic. Facts like these created occasion for the c nosideration of the subject of the Day of Rest, and for asking men and women of all shades of opinion and of all aged to think carefully WHAT IT MEANT TO,,SECULARISE SUNDAY.

It was not the purpose of fps discourse to discuss the questiQu at any len4th of the Di 7ine authority of the ChririAn Sabbath. Ile would proceed rather upon the assumption that they who were met together, were largely agreed that the Day of R-st and Woralaip, known as the Lord's Day, or Sunday, did pow es a good deal of what might very properly be described as Divine authority. It might be true that nowhere in the New Testament did they nod any such literal enactment of the Christina Sabbath as they found in the Old Testa- ment of the new Ss3bath. They had no new Testament equivalent for the fourth commandment, but neither, it alight he said, had they any such literal equivalent for the other nine commandments. No one, however, questioi.ed the reasonableness or the validity of the moral enactments of the Mosaic Conisaandineuts, or their binding nature upon all who believed in God. All of them were precepts which made for good and righteousness between man and God, and between man and man. If that be so in respect of the nine, it might safely be inferred that it was the same with respect to the command for Sabbath keeping. The Changing con- ditions ot life and of humanity might indeed have ren- dered expedient a change in the day for the weekly rest but the principle of such weckly rest-keeping held good as long as human life remained what it ever had been. HUMAN NATURE NEEDED THIS PERIODIC REST . .

from toil. If that be so then a weekly rest day bad heen authorised and required by Him who create,' human nature. It was a law to be honoured and to be obeyed, with all possible respect and obedience by every man, and if so, then every man's duty, so far as ever it was possible, was to see that every other man, as well as himself had opportunity for that same ~bedience. Of , Conte.) there would inevitably be certain limitations to

absolute and literal obedience to this requirement They knew that buinan life could not be lived, human society Could not go on without certain kinds and degrees of work and toil even on the day of rest. But the welfare of the individual, and that of society at large, being involved in the mainten Ince of a weekly rest day, all possible care must be taken to guard it from distur- bance and still more to prevent its destruction. He hai frequently MARVELLED AT THE UTTER SELFISHNESS

so greatly displayed in the maters of recreation and amusement, and especially in the modern Sunday development. Ho had been teld by drivers of brakes and waggonettes that in the summer the Sunday work was the hardest, both for men and horses, of all the work of the week. And it was the hardest because the working-classes made it so. Men, who if they were required to work on Sunday would expect, in many eases even by the rules of their unions would demand extra, or even dnub'e pay, compelled their fellow work- ingmen to toil and slave on Sundays for their pleasure without any thought of extra pay. This was not done of malice, of course, but of sheer thoughtlessness; and this thoughtlessness of others sprang from inordinate thoughtfulness of self. Beyond, howeva, the physical authority for the weekly day of rest which was of local- eulable value to human well-be'ng, there was the still higher and deeper reason of its moral value. The weekly day of rest was God's method of keeping roan in mind of his higher nature. Be ventured to say that the peeplo who began life by Sabbath k eping, by the habitual worstaip of God in Ms house, but who had given up that early habit, bad not in reality profited, but had lost by that transaction. SECULARIZE A MAN AND YOU UNSPIRITUALIZE HIM. Neglect and misuse of Sunday was the bane of multi- tudes to-day. And it was the beginning of moral deterioration in any one who had ever professed to be rAigious. Nowadays the whole of Sunday was deemed too long for religion. This was a danporous tendency and should be steadily resisted. Its trend was towards a chronic irregularity in Sunday observance that would Presently become regular non-observance of Sunday in

Authors of let ere intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor.

1 that on Sunday next, April 7th, (Easter Day) two concerts aredo be giver, by the Band of His Majesty's Royal Grena- dier Guards in the said 'theatre at which the mull charges are to be made tor admission. This continued violation of the law is regarded in a very serious light by the Deputation and by those they represent,particularlv as the Act of Parlia- ment which is thereby contravertied is the only one which provides that Theatres, Music Halls, Voncert Halls 80. shall not be opened for the purpose of amusement on the Lord' Day, as on weekdays. the object which the Deputation has in view is to preserve the weekly Day of Rest for all,and in accordance with the existing law, to prevent the encroach- ment upon it of performances in The dres or Public Halls for profit, gain, or by way of trade. It is therefore, hoped that the Watch Committee will see its way to give such directions as will enema compliance with the law in the future. The Deputation do not wish to su gest that any harsh or irritat- ing measures should be taken, but they are of the opinion that the giving of public entertainments on dundays, for money payment. is a matter of such grave national and local importance that they feel it incumbent upon them to lay their views before the Watch Committee, with a view to securing the observance of the existing law a copy of which will be found on pages -B.and 9 of the accompanying pamphlet. The deputation will it is expected consist of the Rev Canon Willis, Rector of Warrington, the Rev 3 Divies, Vicar of St Ann's ; Rev Francis fi Powell, Vicar of St. James'; Rev W Bracecamp, Curate of nt Peter's; Rev C H Hocken. Wesleyan Minister and Secretary, Evangelical Free Church Council ; Rev Cherie' Finch, Primitive Methodist Minister and Vice- President Free Church Council; Rev E T Reed, Minister of Emmanuel Church, and possibly, others. The Act of Parliament referred to is that which 12 com- monly known as "The Lord's Day Act" viz Geo. 111. 'o 49.. by which "it is enacteddhat any house room or other place which shall be opened or used for public entertainment or amusement, or for publicly debating on any subject what- soever on any part Of the Lord's-lay, called Sunday, and to svhion persons shall oe admitted by is may nent of money, or by tickets sold for money, shall be deemed a dlsorderl house or place. and the keeper thereof shall forfeit £2OO for every day that such house. room, or plage shall be opened or used as aforesaid on the Lord's-day, to such person as will sue for the same, and be otherwise punishable as in cases of dis- orderly houses; and the porrsons managing or conducting such entertainment or amusement on the Lord'r-day, or acting as master of the ceremonies there, or as Moderator, president. or chairman of any such meeting for public debate on the Lord's-day, shall likewise for every such offence for- feit £lOO to such persons as will see for the same. And every door-keeper, servant, or other person who shall collect or receive money or tickets from perilous assembling at .uch house, or who shall deliver out tickets for admitting persona thereto, shall also forfeit £50.4 nee. 3.—Any person advertising such nubile entertainment or amusement, or .meeting or printing or publishing any such advertisement, shall forfeit £so.*. . .

Dear Mr. lieed,—lt is very uncettain whether I shall be able to form part of the deputation to-morrow, but the object has my entire sympathy and support. As I understand it, this is no move to bring legal pressure to bear upon religious institutions .vhich should rely as much as possible on religious Sanctions alone. Neither is it an attempt to prevent people enjoying such Sunday recreation as their consciences will permit, and their laclinations desire. It is, I think, wise.: to leave people as unfettered as possible in their method of Sunday ot.servance. But the Lords Day of Rest has become one of the greatest blessings to our natiOn,and every attempt to destroy so beneficent an institution should be strenuously resisted. It is n ea, safeguarded by statute. Remove this statute and the Institution Is in danger of being wrecke I. It will be seen therefore that we are merely anxious to continue this statutory protection to what has been and still is. so priceless a boon to thee .mmunity. And we, with alt respect. and may I add, in full confidence ?—appeal to our civic authorities not to abow s., useful a law to become a dead letter. I thank you very much for asking me to associate with you and others in so important and pressing a matter. Very truly, yours

" P J.'s referenoe to the obvious fact that "Brains are not mmopolised by any class " was shown most die- tinctiy at the recent election by a large number of work- ing men who in spite of the appeals made to their Trades Unionist oreju lime did not 883 the wisdom of trying to 8: a round man into a square hole and consequently voted as their" brains" and intelligence dictated ? Your correspondent further alive that I seem to rejoice in the fact of this deplorable state of mind of his fellow townsmen. What does he mean by this suggestive sen- tence? Dues be mean that rhe working men of this town are so apathetic and indifferent to their own and to their wives' and children's interests as not to care how their town is governed. If this is what be means, then I tell him he is libelling and that most grossly the work- ing men of Warrington. There is in every town a certain amount of ignorance and lack of interest in local govern- ment, but because the people here do not choose to accept as gospel the hare-brained chatter of every \ irrespoesib!e demagogue who makes up for his lack of argumm,. I

()Nee more the main The Situation in. South Africa, inte,est in the war i 3 trans- ferred from the military te the political sphere, Although the campaign drags its slew length along, this has become the day of small things in matters military. Successes of a petty sort continue to attend the British operations in various parts of South Africa; but, though the result is- to add to the tale of losses of brave lives on both sides, nothing decisive seems to be effected by these small achievements. It is only to the gradual exhaustion of the Boers, apparently, that we must look for the end which appears yet a great way off. Meantime, the announcement that Sir ALERED MILNER is Doming home for a brief holiday fias led to a variety ei speculations as to, the "true inwardness" of this movement. It is said in some quarters that the pretext of a holiday is only put forward to cover the retirement of the High Commissioner, and that he is not likely to return to South Africa. It may be that this suggestioa is only made bY those to whom the wish is father to thc thought. Doubtless Sir ALFRED needs a holiday. The events of the past two years, in which he has played a leading party must have been even painfuly trying to him. Ile is as much disliked and denounced by one party f° the Cape, and by those who support it in this country, as he is admired and belauded by another; and the fact that he has not given entire satisfac• tion to either party may be put down to well-in' tentioned, but not wholly successful, efforts t° steer an even course between them. It may net be Sir Altred's fault that he is not persona grata to the Afrikanders and their friends. He has ln^d a policy to carry out which was naturally distastP- ful to them, and, as usual in such cases, the aver sion which a distasteful policy occasions to its opponents has concentrated itself upon the, certainly devotedyand possibly innocent,head of its instrument. Eeen Sir ALFRED MILNER'S strongest critics credit him with honesty, sincerity, patriot' ism, and the desire to do his duty according to his lights. To a change in the South African High Consmissionership just now there may be urged ell' the objections covered by the warning against swapping horses while crossing a stream. At the same time, if the appointment of a ins° less committed to a policy which has not so f"t proved successful in securing the submission of ths Boor leaders still in the field, should smooth Os way to an early and honourable peace, and facili" tate a satisfactory settlement, then a course whie/3 otherwise would be of questionable expedieriel may be abundantly warranted. It has fallen to the lot of ' A Novel Mansion the LORD MAYOR of Lon- House Appeal. don to make many and various appeals on behalf of deserving chant- able and benevqlent objects. As a rule such appeals meet with a generous response. Not a few of them are made to the nation at large, for the LORD Mevon,in addition to his other civic and social functions, has come to be regarded as a sort of national almoner. To shew how handsomely

the country esponds to appeals which touch its heart, he has just reminded it that the combined. funds in aid of the sufferers by the Transvaal war nod the Indi,,n famine have reached over £a.500,000. Probably even that amount would have been exceeded did the public place entire confidence in the wise administration and distri- bution of these funds. Lord Mayor GREEN has Just made an appal somewhat out of the common. It is on behalf ,of the Mansion House Couneilion thelDwellings of the Poor, an organisation which essays, if not to solve the great housing pro- blem, at all events to assist in its solution. This council was formed eighteen years ago, nod its object, in one expressive word, is— 'Slumming." L idies and gentlemen who are Members make house-to-house visitations in poor neighbourhoods and bring to light cases of over- crowding and insanitation. Not only are these brought to the notice of public authorities, but dyke and assistance are given to the inmates of %limit habitations as to the purification and improve- 'mint of their dwellings. Six public inquiries by the Local Government Board and three by the London County Council have been held on the rePresentation of CPIs Mansion House Council and other good work has been done. But it cannot live entirely without funds, and it has now alhausted a legacy of £5,000 from the Berridge Trust, made to it in 1892. Hence an appeal is kade to Londoners to assist this important voluti- ,tary work towards the redemption of their city 'mom its slums. It has been well remarked that no single measure will entirely solve the wide and complicated problem of the housing of the poor.l batever the Legislature may do, whatever powers local authorities may have conferred upon them, there will always be much to be done atnong the slum dwellers themselves, on whom largely rests the sanitary condition of their dwell- ings. With the most approved legislation, there will be need here for the operations of educational and moral agencies. The subject, too, is mixed 11p.with the drink question, for there is abundant evidence that the demoralisation caused by the drinking habits of the poorer classes is both a cause and an effect of overcrowding and insanitary conditions. In the campaign against the "three 133 " of our slums—" dirt, drunkenness and disease "—there is, therefore, a wide field for vol- untary efforts like that of the Mansion House Council on the Dwellings of the poor. In sup- Porting the novel appeal Of the Lon]) MAYOR for hinds to help this Council, we would also corn- end similar agencies elsewhere, and it would be Well if councils on the model of that of the London Ransion H ,use- could be established in many towns and vi 'ages where they do not already

Mr. Stead speaks very plainly in this month's "Review of Reviews," and his opinions on many' topics make interesting reading. We are inclined to agree with him when he calls Mr. Chamberlain a " marplot " and lays the entire blame for the failure of the peace negotiations at his door. "Joe's" interference has been costly, and is likely to be increasingly so, for the Boers show no signs of becoming submissive to British rule of their own accord. Kitchener's concessions, which were digni- fied and statesmanlike, were ruthlessly struck out and blnntly called " inconceivably mischievous " by Britain's irrepresSihle God of War, who seems determined not to ha u peace at ally price. Thus far Mr Stead's views and our own entirely agree, bat when he goes on to speak of the war itself then we feel the necessity for dis.lssociating ourselves from his remarks. Somehow Mr Stead's connec- tion with the conciliation Committee and the S:op the War Committee, seems to have biassed his intellect to an incredible degree, and rendered it quite impossible for him to view the progress of the SOuth African Campaign with the calmness and dispassionateness which, as a journalist, he should possess pre-eminently. Even in a casual reference Mr. Stead's bitterness is apparent. For instance, "General French has been doing sheep- stealing on a large scale, and the war against women and children goes merrily on." Such senti- ments do their author no credit, but tempt even those who have been his admirers to stigmatise him unpatriotic and "Pro-Boer."

Provincial Volunteer officers and men alike must have been somewhat disappointed by the last War Office order relating to encampments, which had been awaited with no little anxiety. In paragraph 5, class 1, the allowance for officers is set down at 8s per day, while 2s 61 will be allowed for members of other ranks. The officers' allowance of eight shillings will by no means cover his expenses It is supposed, therefore that he will have to do as so many Volunteer officers have done before,namely, put his hand into his pocket and pay the difference. It may be early yet to enter into mintr,e calcula- tions, and we shall certainly know more when the experiment has been male and officirs and men have their version ot the story to offer. But it is quite certain that commanding officers will not con- Moue to take their battalions into camp at a pecuni- ary loes to themselves,in addition to having to pay a considerable item t)wards their own personal expenses. litoreover,the experience of the men who have had previous knlwledge of camp-life is that half-a-crown will not nearly suffice, and, besides this, no arrangement appears to have been made / for paying the men while in camp. The camps can scarcely prove attractive or successful unless the man are paid so that their domestic affairs may be kept in order daring their temporary abs ice. Volunteers will readily comply with the more stringent regulations that have been ordered in regard to camp duties; but they cannot be expected to show the same degree of readiness to attend the encampment for eight, tea, or fourteen days with- out some assurance that in the meantime the wives and little ones are not likely to auffr for want of anything. It is alsJ certain that no monetary assist- ance can be forwarded to the latter out of the thirty pence per day allowed for maintenance while in camp.

The bitter cry of the professional musician is heard again and louder than ever. The musical season has been a bad one, and for all that can be seen worse still may remain ahead. The instrumen- talist laments his loss of occupation, but his case is not so bad as that of the vocalist, for many of whose class there is no remunerative employment to be obtained. In particular, engagements at private houses are said to have become a diminish- ing quantity—in fact, almost to have disappeared. Possibly the improved training which can now be obtained by the few, at however low an ebb musical instruction for the general mass of the people may have fallen, is to some extent responsible for putting the mitor professional in diminished request. It would appear, how- ever, that a more considerable factor in depressing the musical market is the extent to which the system of free performances has grown. The hostess nowadays, It is complained, instead of holding a musical gathering pure ar,d simple, in very many cases invites her friends to a benefit on behalf of the funds of some war charity,sorne local hospital, or SOMA other deserving object,and thinks herself, in consequence, entitled to request the services of a few accomplished singers, though of minor reputation, on behalf of this good cause. With their ever ready sympathy the musicians fall into the trap, hoping,perhaps, that paying engage- ments may result, and get as reward a number of invitations to assist gratis in similar functions elsewhere. It is a hard case indeed, if there is no exaggeration in the complaint, especially as musicians are in the very nature of things pre- cluded from effective combination of a Trade- Union character.

A million a year on football ! That is the amount which Ragland has been spending on this form of pastime. In the current number of "Blackwool " there is so article on the " Football Nations,"which app-are to be intended to show that England is no longer the monopolist of what has hitherto been recogn6ei as one of our national games. Parisians have for some years coquetted with the Rugby sport but only in ii haV-heerted way, and they have yet to realize its fascination uncl.r the Association code, ' when played by first-class teams. It is re- gretted that the teams which have until now crossed the Channel from this country , , have generally been of a third or fourth-rate quality. The success of the game la Germany is considered largely due to the encouragement given by the Emperor. No German played football twenty years since, but now, says the writer in I question, there are some fifteen thousand regular players in the Fatherland. The still young Emperor is credited also wiih encmraging other forms of English sport, such as rowing, and love-and-bye we shall have international chellengeri of quite a differ- ent character from those to which we nave already been accustomed. Per laps the sooner the better, the reader may say.

--- A question of precedence is troubling Birming- ham—whether in procession the City magistrates should walk before the City Councillors, or the City Councillors should walk before. The point arose in connection with a memorial service for the late Queen Victoria, and has been troubling the authorities ever since. On that occasion the Lord Mayor, of course, headed the line; but the Town Clerk placed the City Councillors next him, and the administrators of law and order had third positim. Inquiries have since been made, and it has since been ascertained that the same custom of arrangement prevails at Liverpool, Bristol and Bradtord ; while on the other hand,. Glasgow, Manchester, Nottingham, Leicester, Leeds and York give precedence to the magistrates. The apologists for the Councillors ask whether the Lord Mayor heads a procession as Lord Mayor or as Chief Magistrate of the City, and they claim that the obvious answer resolves the point. It must be plain, however, that the magistracy is the higher dignity because it is per- manent, and it touches the liberty of the subject. It may be said that a Council, by their regulations, control the magistrates individually in many of their affairs; but these regulations would be use- less unless magistrates were prepated to enforce them. The magistrates, when there is any ad- ministration to be done, form the higher authority. As a rule, too, they are older men, and it is pleasant to see respect given to age. The matter is of very little consequence, except that so long as the law is administered to any extent by justices of the peace, it is well they should be given respect, for then, if warnings have weight, they will not need to make their sentencss quite so heavy. The time will come, however, when stipendiaries will do all the work.

As bearing upon the hold which the most popular games exercise on some even of our highly placed fellow-countrymen, one is reminded that Lord Alverstone has became a convert to the benefits of golf, and is said to play just now almost daily at Colwyn Bay. Mr A Balfour has described golf as "one of the greatest solaces of life," while the Lord Chief Justice has already expressed the opinion that goifing is the most "reposeful " recreation possible for a busy man. Mr Balfour had the assurance once to say, at Chiselhur.t, that he watched with satisfaction the gradual " Scotifica- tion "of England by the great golfing propaganda The cult of the flying ball certainly appears to have taken deep root among us. For a long time it made but little, if any progress, but this cannot truth- fully be said of it now-a-day.

MATTERS OF MOMENT. A Provincial Letter.

Next to the Census, the official returns calculated to produce the most interest in the public mind are those of the Registrar-General relating to birth, marriages, and d-aths in England and Wales, pub- lished this week although bearing only upon the year 1899 It had been feared in some quarters that the marriage-rate was declining, but us a matter of fact marria .e prospects are brighter than they have , been for some years Marriages registered during I the year numbered 262,134. corresponding to a rate of 16.5 persons married per thousand of the popula- tion, and this is ths highest rate recorded since' 1876 The increase, moreover, has been general throughout the country,a fact which throws a side- light upon our commercial and industrial pros- perity. The counties showing the greatest number of illiterates are Cornwall, Somerset, Shropshire,and Suffolk. But there are darker spots still, namely Monmouth, and both North and South \Vales. It seems almost incredible that at the close of the nineteenth century so many people should have entered the ma i tinge state by affixing" crosses "to the register as the returns show. The death-rate remains practically unchanged, at 183 per thou- sand. But infant mortality continues to mount up, being twelve per cent higher than in he previous ten year,. This is, perhaps, not surprising, seeing that at inquest after inquest some of the most amazing revelations are made as to the way in which bahies are sametime., fe t,and that time after time coroners actually find it necessary to inform the mother how she ought to have treated her own infant In the manufacturing distric s,of course,the labour question comes in, and has yet to be dealt with by the Legislature. But this is not all, since, if certain of these cases cannot be brought under the existing criminal law, hat law will have to be amended so as to admit and allow them to be pro- perly dealt with,

Reference has been made to our " commercial and industrial prosperity." When the Associated Chambers of Commerce were assembled a menth since, business men were told on the staistical authority of Mr. Holt Schooling that our trade for many years had been declining; and this week the same authority comes forward with a damaging assault upon the co 13forting doctrine long cherished in many circles that " trade follows the flag,' in a paper in which he examines the condition and ten- dencies of our export trade, It is news, of course, to most of us. The year 1900 may have been better than 1899 and 1896 than 1895 but taking trade in quinquennial periods Mr Schooling finds there has been a steady and continuous decrease in our exports during the past fifteen years, and that the fall extends to all our leading articles of export except coal. it is especially marked in woollen and worsted manufactures, cottm goods, iron and steel, linen and leather products, and as the popula- tion has in the meantime g ne on increasing, the relative is really greater than the actual decline.

RUNCORN NOTES. [BY A. CORRESPONDRNT.]

To the sensible, it le s—elf-e—vident that the object of Coroner's inquests is to arrive FA the cause which brings about death with an ultinlate view of doing something by way of recommendation—especially in the base of accidents—that may remedy the causes of disaster. Such, however, does not appear to be the view taken by Runcorn juries, else, we would long ago have had something done to lessen the casualty list which is provided by the "dangers of the Canal." A lamentable case comes before us this week, from which we learn that a fireman un- doubtedly the worse for liquor ventures down to the docks and eventually finds a wa'ery grave in the Weston Canal. Surely it should be the object of the jury to find out whether the" accident" is caused by culpable neglect on the part of the unfortunate deceased or of the Ship Canal Comoany in not pro- viding sufficient light; ancl,being satisfied on either point I feel sure any strong recommendation made by a Runcorn jury would be immediately met.

Personally I incline to the belief that if a Canal Constable was put on the watch, say one hour after sunset, near the approaches to the docks, and empowered to prevent any drunken sailors from going to their boats by this dangerous route, much would be done to prevent a recurrence of these of ten repeated calamities. Of my own knowledge, lam aware that the local members of the Cheshire Con- stabulary do a very large and us-ful work in this direction, but the nature of the Canal footpaths being private prevents them from doing it with the Perfectness the extreme largeneFs of the casualty list demands I shall be pleased to ventilate any suggestions that may lead to bringing about a diminution in their number.

The annual meeting of the Runcorn Urban Dis- trict Council, unanimously elected Mr R H Posnett, of "The Mount." Runcorn. to its chairmanship; and Mr W Handley (the retiring chairman) according to precedent, wa3 unanimously elected vice-chairman. Mr Posnett's election was, indeed, a surprise, as it was generally believed that Mr J Pritchard ( who has long deserved the office) would this year have been prevailed upon to stand. Mr Pritchard what- ever he may think of the matter, ought to be made Chairman, as no one holds a better claim to the rights of the "190 g and honourable succession" than he. However, I will say no more, but deal with Mr Posnett. That he is young, able, and will- ing to work for the benefit of the town is uncon- trovertible. These three qualities go far in these days of" high-pressure," and we shall expect much. His elevation has been very rapid,and follows hardly more than eighteen months experience as a coun- illor. He himself lays no pretentious to a title to the honour.and I find difficulty in tracing his public career to come across anything which should cause the Council to elect him as their chairman, save that he is straightforward, and well-grounded in sound principles He has, however, yet to win his "spurs" in the public service, and it would appear as if the Council have honoured him for what he is I about to do rather than for what be has done.

I congratulate him, heartily upon gaining such a nistinction in so sbort a period, and I congratulate the town, and the broad-minded councillors who so liberally recognise merit, whether in a native or in one who has made the town that of his adoption. On Mr Pas/WV* own showing his career, during the last 13 years. smatters somewhat of "Dick Whittington';of:old, botil sincerely hope his future

A FAINIILY'S SAD PLIGUT AT P,UNC3aN.

Messrs J Pritchard anu B unison, whilst sitting at the Runcorn Police Court on Thursday morning had a very sA 1 story told them by a married couple, named Charles Richard and Sarah Jana Williams, ' who were brought betore them to answer to a charge of neglect and cruelty to their two children aged respectively 3 years, and 18 months —Con- stables Bowyer and Wilding illustrated how they found the defendants about 2 o'clock that very morning , huddled up with their children in the " forecastle" of an old narrow boat, at the bottom of the Vine Yard. Runclrn. They were ' trying to sleep on the floor of the boat, without bedding or covering. The children were wretchedly clad,and were shivering with cold. The smell from them was also very objectionable, and they were very vermiaous.—Prisoners elected to be summarily dealt with, and gave an outline of their destitution which both &lege(' was caused by no fault of their own. They had inten led seeing the relieving officer the previous day, but when they found the shelter of the boat, they thought it would do the next day It was now their intention to take the children to the Workhouse until they could find a place for them.—The Magistrates Clerk : That will be a very nice arrangement from your point of view.—The Magistrates eventually decided to bind prisoners over in 40; recognisance to come up for judgment when called upon, and discharged them on the understanding they went to the Workhouse and took the children with them.

Witness was quite sure that prisoner had taken the money. Wit- ness had had some drink, but he knew what was going on.—Other Poliedi witnesses corroborated , with respect to the theft, and prisoner was com- mitted to the Sessions for trial.

FOOTBALL CLUB. Retrospect of the ,Season, 1900--1901. Naturally, one is not quite so jubilant in begin- ning the task of fighting Runcorn's football bat- tles over again, as was the case when the task fell my way last season. But it has to be done, soberly, sensibly, and exhaustively, for are not you, my readers, impatient to tramp along the glorious path the club has traced even this season ; and having the signposts, as it were, set up along your route, the cords of memory net unaided by imagination will enable your mind's eye to picture those de- lightful games of which you were an admiring witness.

The League campaign opened with the Birrow fifteen at, Runcorn, and resulted in Runcorn win- nine, by 10 points to 5; certainly not e trilliant gait; and no one wondered when the following week saw last year's champions fall at Swinton, though the:largeness of the score against them caused comment enough. The remainder of Sep- tember and the ficst week of October were produc- tive of four wins, Broughton (h), St Helens (a), .Millotn (a), and Oldhein (la) these teams being van- quished by an aggregate of 28 pointe to 2 points (a goal secured by Oldham). The mach against Oldham was ale.> remarkable, in- asmuch as neithcr side scored tries ; the result being made up of 3 goals, kicked by Houghton (6 points), and the one which the Oldham captain secured. During all this thno,to the team displayed a steady form, and went about their matches with a tu•inese like manner, that spoke somewhat of the preceding season's form. October 13th saw the great tussle over at Widnes; and as Runcorn had the preceding eaturday played Oldham to a standstill, iVidnes—who by the way were showing wretched form—were considered as good as beaten. Local feeling ran high, and the loetbridge of the London and Horeb Western Rail- , way Company, had a good innings that Saturday, The game was a keen one, and resulted ha Widnes winning by 10 points to nil. The Runcorn players returned from Widnes a bit ashamed of themselves but fully determined to be 'level" on the next encounter. This event came off during the Easter holidays, but Widnes showed such improved form that Runcorn only just succeeded in getting back their own, the score being Runcorn 13 points to 3 points. Three wins followed, viz, Warrington, Stockport, and Wigan. The match with Warrington was indeed a capital one and deserves to rank high in the season's games. Both teams were, perhaps,at their bast, and although Runcorn carried off the palm with 10 points to 8 points, it might just as easily have been reversed. The following Saturday, the match of the season, came off at Salford. Runcorn being beaten just by a point —(11-10)—although they played a much superior game to their opponents. Barrow the following week, further discomfited Runcorn, and let the Senior champions know they were little better than the Second Division champions. It was an object lesson to the league, and indicated that the aspiring juniors would have to be reckoned with. December 15th to February 2nd inclusive, proved a successful period for Runcorn, and therein nine consecutive victories were registered, includ- ing : Stockport (h). Wigan (a), Warrington (a), Leigh (h), Rochdale Hornets (a), Millom (h), Leigh (a), Salford and Swinton (h). During this period Runcorn scored 18 tries, 18 goals, 90 points; against their opponents 2 goals, 3 tries, 13 points, and ex- hibited a form equal to any of their best days. In- deed those were" happy days" wherein to meet a Runcorn man, he was such a sunny, cheerful' creature. Broughton Rangers registered the next defeat, securirg a well-earned victory at BreughtOn of 7 points to 2 points. St Helens were beaten the following Saturday, and the next match which was with Oldham saw Runcorn lose their last points in the league; the home team winning by 11-3. The remaining two matches, against Widnes and Roch- dale, came off during the holidays,and only enabled Runcorn to better their average, by securing 39 pints egain4 three points. Briefly summed up Runcorn take third place in the League, with the following record : Played, 26; won 20, lost 6, drawn 0, points for, 45 goals 50 trifle 240 points; points against 14 goals, 24 tries, 100 points. As compared with last year the figures ehow the Runcorn defence to be somewhat weakened, for they won the cup with an average of 7'03, scoring 232 points against 33 points. On this point I shall have a little more to say when dis- cussing the playere.

A LAN DMA RK ALREADK.

The year 1901 has already become a landmark in the h:atory of tht United Kingdom, and sorrow tias Marked With unwonted universal:4 the whole of the nations of the Earth. Cable Makers Busy—Scarcity of Guttapercha.

Cable construction is going ahead at such a rate says the " Daly Express " that there is some pros- pect of a shortage in the supply of guttapercha. Borneo, Sumatra, and other islands in the Far East are the present sources of supply, and Singapore the chief centre from which all the supplies are re- distributed, to the cable makers. England, France, and Germany are all engaged in cable-laying on a great scale, the two latter with a view to disputing the supremacy of England under as well as over the sea. England's present cable schemes include the construction of the all-British cable across the Pacific, and a new cable from Africa to Australia—some 15,030 miles in all. For these two cables the contracts are I already Work has commenced on the Afro- Australian cable, and the making of the Pacific cable will begin next year. Having accepted the contract, it is reasonable t,) suppose that the English firm which is laying these two cables has enough guttrpercha in prospect to complete them. But there will not be much left over for France and Germany to buy after British requirements have been sAtiefied In the opinion of one great manu- facturer, it will not be impossible for France and Germany to get enough guttapercha to complete the schemes they have in hand, but they will have to wait a long time and pay a big price.

Oldham Defeated by Batley.

There was an excellent attendance of 15,000 at Huddersfield on Saturday to witness the match between Oldham and Batley.and the proceeds amounted to £B9O. The terraces were filled and those who found places in the covered stands saw before them the imposing spectacle of a wall of people such as can be built up on no other football eround in the county, save, per- haps, at the Crystal Palace. Much greater crowds are often huddled together at ordinary Saturday afternoon football matches, but the ken thousand comfartably accommodated on the Hucdersfield hillside filled the eye far more impressively than double the number would have done packed together on a structure of necessarily against the weather Fortunately a for the majority pro- s tnot re 9.3ired.To visitors from Manchester and from Leeds this was a welcome limited ec pro- tection surprise. The "place" was very near the half-sway line, but it was directly in front of goal. and there was the wied to be counted On, W. Davies was the kicker, and he scored with apparent ease. Ten minutes later pre- cisely the same sort ot thisg happened again. lii the same position Mr. Bruckshsw blew his whistle for "legs up in the scrum." and again Davies placed a goal. Tuns Batley obtained a lead of four points without any particular display of merit, beyond that of the individual player who took the place kicks. For a time the teams struggled hard and unsuc- cessfully. On a dry ground it was reasonable to expect some good three-cleaner nlay, but those vs ho did expect ie were disappointed. Only once in the first half was there a really stirring passage of ‘xchanges. In this Huzzey and Fletcher shared, and so well did they man- age the ball that thee carried the game from their own half well into the Batley " twenty-five" before they were stopped. Oldham could gain nothing by kicking or punting because of the wind, and it was difficult for them te field the ball because of the sun. Stubborn &feroci. they offered, and there seemed a prospect that the interval might be reached without further scoring. But again the unexpected happened. Fletcher, puzzled by the MO, misised the ball, and Batley got up from a scrummage in close quarters. Th'e Batley halves were smart and lucky. Midgley snapped up the ball and dodged two opponents. A third collared him, and he passed the ball ti his nelleague. Oakland in turn was tackled, but be grounded the ball over the line as he fell. From this try Davies made another succ-ssful place kick. and Batley had a lead of nine points at the interval. The character of the game aftetwards changed very considerably. Batley, while the wind was with them, had forced the pace at every possible opportunity, and they bad of course somewhat wearied their rivals. But' having secured a lead, they went to the other extreme, and played in a leisurely fashion which provoked the Lancashire spectators to sheuts of ireny. Their leisurely methods, however, were only in the direction of filling up time. In defensive play they showed excellent form, and pe,haps their greatest triumph was in the complete manner in which they mastered and broke down the Oldham methods of attack. The forwards had most to do. and the Batley forwards were undoubtedly the smarter pack, especially in their footwork. One success only rewarded Oldham for much hard fighting. Soon after the game was resumed, Mr Bruckshaw penalised a Batley .half for not putting the ball straight into the serum; and from the place Huzzey kicked a goal. There was no other score; only two or three abortive attempts. Of these Dayies's drop kick was nearest to success. & really exciting spell bf play rounded off the game. Oldham appeared to get the measure of the op- posing backs:and scruminages very near to the line were frequent. But here a knock—on and there a failure to take a pass spoiled every chance opening, and in the end the result was a victory for Batley by three goals and one try (nine points) to one goal (two points).

THE COST OF WAR. From the day when Av.hilles fasted three days and nights over the body of PArce'us, slain in the ten years siege before the stout walls of Troy, have we learned of war's sorrows.

The Duke of Wellington wept like a child when he surveyed the bloody. bard-won field of Waterloo. Methuen, it is said, broke down at the ghastly sight of Magersfontein, and returned to his tent that the men might not witness his grief. " Who dead is must he buried," say s Homer, and, we might add," who ill is must die." So thought Piiirate 'John Smith, living now at 19. South tikbridge-street, Bu.tcm-m.-Trent. The "Aurania " steamed out to the Cape on December 27th, 1899, with this soldier aboard her (that was before she seamed back with the gallant C.l.V.'s) and in telling me of his trials, on August 29 h, 1900, Private Smith says that that part of his duty was plea,iant enough. " Shortly after Earl Roberts' famous march into Kronstadt, we were stationed between that town and Jacobsdal to guard the line of communication. Towards the end of April I was laid low with that fell dieease enteric fever.

"There were hundreds of men down with it. It has I laid law more men than all the shot and shell that was I ever fired by Boer Artillery. For several weeks I was in the fever h3spltsl. Mysutnach was converted into a manufactory of torturing pains and I grew as weak and helpless as a new-born babe. "1 was for days unconscious, but gradually recovered sufficiently to be invalided home." 1 Yet the suffering of Private Smith was not over. "I went aboard the Hospital Ship ' Ninevah 'in June," he says, "and shortly afterwards sailed for home. During my short stay in South Afiica, I lost over 20lbs in weight. Nothing that I took relieved the constant vomiting, or increased my strength." " After," he says, I had been home a few days, my brother, who is an engio driver on tho Midland Rail- way— not of an arntortred train—advieed me to try Mother Seigel's Syrup, I got a bottle. I found it was doing me good. The vomiting ceased, and I was able to enjoy all kinds of food. My appetite is now enor- mous."

"Altogether I have taken tour bottles of Mother Sc;gel's Sup, and gladly give you permission to use and publish toy statement. This syrup is a fine tonic and a great medicine for the stcmaoh. I can especially reccmmend it to soldiers re ing from South Africa in feeble health."
