Interesting Proceedings.

Last week's meeing'of the Warrington Board of Guardians was the last of the triennial period, and an interesting feature in connection with it was the presentation of a clock to Mr William Owen and of a silver rose bowl; to •Mr William Houghton, in recognition of their services in connection with the building of the new Workhouse Infirmary. The presents had been supplied by Messrs Eustance and Company. Mr Owen's present was an English bracket chime clock in old carved oak case,chiming the Cambridge chimes on eight bells and the West- minster chimes on four beautifull3 toned Cathedral gongs. The clock is mounted on a massive carved oak bradket and bears the following inscription "Presented to William Owen, Esq„ F. R.1.8.A. by the Guardians of the Warrington Union on the corapletien of the new Infirmary,l9oo " Tbe silver rose bowl presented to Mr William Houghton was of Louis XIV design with panels of flowers chased In relief en the'body of the bowl and a slroll border ot roses and acanthus leaves on the pedestal. The bowl is mounted on an ebony plinth and is a very handsome work of art. Mr A J JOLLEY presided, and there was a full attend. anoe of members. The CHAIRMAN said that some time ago a recommen., &SUM was submitted to the lutirmary Qopamittee, and met with a very cordial response. That recointnenda- tion was to the effect that the omardians should do s unething as a body to recognize the services rendered to them bp the architect—Mr William Owen, in connec- tion with the erection of the new Infirmary. The pro- posal was received in a most cordial spirit, and it was decided that the Guardians, out of private resources, should endeavour in some way to express to Mr William Owen their vary strong appreciation of the many services he had rendered to them in connection with the building of the new Infirmary—services which sometimes had fallen outside the limits of his architectural duties, but which had always been rendered most cheerfully. Any informa, ion required had always been given with promp- titude, and tneir relations together had been of a most satisfactory character. (Hear, hear.) Of course Mr Owen had received the usual professional remunera- tion for his services but altogether owside that, it had been unanimously felt that some further recognition should be made. The spirit of goodwill bad materi4l- - itself in the form of a handsome clock which the members saw before them. He had now very great pleasure in asking, Mr Houghton to formally present the clock to Al, Owen. _ _

Mr HouciatoN said he had often felt proud of his position as a member of the Board but he bad never felt prouder than he did on the present occasion. Be was obliged to the Chairman for the preface he had just made, for it relieved him of that duty. The ques- tion of building the new infirmary was no new thing to those who had been on the B iard for any number of years, for it had been in agitation eight or ten Years. The Local Government B )ard began to put pressure upon them as to making better provision for the sick and infirm It was a matter which wanted great consideration and it was thought po,sible that the old place might be • modified to such an extent as to meet the requirements of the Local Government Board Af'er plans had been made it was found that it would be uterly impossible to do this, and they had to set about to prepare for the new building which had now become such a valuable adjunct to the Werkhouse. Mr. Owen was instructed to prepare the plans, and some time elapsed before they were ready, but the time was well spent by Mr Owen in obtaining every information in order to make the Infirmary as modern and up-to-date as it was possible for it to be. Mr 0 Weill was after wards instructor] to go on with the work and a committee was formed to deliberate with him. That committee found its work very much larger than they had at first expected. Frequent meetings had to be held and much ensigns; thought was given to the matter A great deal of it was relieved to a considerable extent by the courtesy ot Mr Owen who did his utmost to assist the committee. Even architects could do their work in more ways than one—they siou'd do it in the ordinary way and also in an exactly opposite manner. At the conclushn of the work, mainly owing to Mr. 0 wen's ability, when the accounts came to be made out, they were not qitite as mueh as they expected or as they had allowed for. In many under- takings the opposite had been the case and it was a pleasant finale to the work of the committee when the undertsking was completed. When the proposal to make a presentation to Mr Owen was mooted, it was take, up unanimously by the committee and afterwards by the Board and th,t beautiful clock which they saw before them was decided upon by the Selection Com- mittee. He did not know of anything that could be more useful than a good clock. The one before them was of English make and lie thought it would prove a sold ona Be hoped the gift would find a Vane in Mr Owen's home. The clock struck the hours and chimed the quarters and he hoped Mr Owen would live long to listen to these chimes, and that when his eyes fell on

that inscription it would excite pleasant memories in hiS breast. When he had ceased to listen to it he (Mr Houghton) hopod that a long line of future generations might listen to its beautiful chimes, and that the inscription would remind them of their ancestor and his work. (Applatise.) Mr. OwEN, in accepting the gilt, said he hed been in the habit of attending that room for 25 or 27 years, but he did not know that he ever felt so nervous before. He thought, for once, that he would like the reporters to be absent, although he agreed with what Mrs Eielsten had said with referenee to their exclusion at the Board meet- ings, and if he might add a word in parantheeiii, he was s rry Mrs Elelsten was leaving the Board. He felt that he could not adequatelythank them for such a handamme and cheerful recognition of his serviees—services, which as the Chairman very properly feinted out, had been paid for. He appreciated most fully—tvne could appre- ciate more fully—the kindly • sentiments which had been the motive in their minds in presenting that very handsome „clock to. him He did not know that there ivatt ady incentive which led an official to serve them better than feeling that they reposed in him trust and confid-nee. They might depend upon ithat whenever they had an official in whom they reposed trust and confidence it was better for them to show it. The goodwill and motives which had promoted that gift to him more than doubled and trebled the value of the payments he had received for the work he had performed He had served the Board fora long time, probably 27 years, and he thought he was the third or feurth oldest officer, It spoke well for the Board—be did not wish to cast any reflee.tion which would Rinw "upnn themselves—that they were able to keep together their servants for more than a quarter of ,a century. If the Board continued to show its appre- ciation of its officers in this manner the Board would have them sticking to them like glue. (Laughter.) Ho greatly appreciated the gift. Ni better gift could be given to a man who rather prided himself on hie punctuality. Whenever he made an appointment; whether it was with a working man or any other person be always endeavoured to keep it, and now he should have no possible excuse. He could assure them that the clock would have a very prominent position in his home, and as he listened to the chimes he should always thick of the pleasant and happy times spent with the Board of Guardians. (Applause.) Mr. PILLING said the members of the Board would remember that at the last meeting when the presenta- tion to Mr. Owen was mentioned the Guardians then present came to the conclusion, after some remarks by the Rev J H Ridiette that the services rf Mr William Houghton, as Chairman of the Infirmary Committee ought also to be recvnizsd. The suggestion was pot to the meeting and heartily approved of. A sub-committee was aPpointed for the purpose of preparing the presen- tation and the result of their work was the silver rose bowl which the ,'inembers saw upon the table. He would ask the Chairman to make the pretyntation. The CHAIRMAN sail it was with very groat pleasure that he dischareed the duty. Mr Houghton was one of tio most med-nt of men, in fact he believed there was only one other Guardian who was more modest, and that was himsef. (Laughter). Every member of the Board concurred in the suggestion that Mr Houghton's services as chairman of the Infirmary Buildings Com- mittee should receive some tangible recognition. He ,hoped the form it had taken would be to the taste of Mr Houghton, and th•t he would accept it in the spirit in which it was offered, as an expressson of the sincere respect mad, regard which his attention to duty had inspired among the rest of his colleagues. (Applause.) Mr. WILLIAM FIOuGHTON said the Board's apprecia- tion of his efforts was more than he had ever imagined. He never thought of a present of any kind, much less, such a handsome and useful present. There were others on the committee who had done more for the new in- firmary because they possessed more ability. The in- firmary was now complete, and formed a fitting com- pletion to what he should like to describe as the reform lera in the history of the Wartington Union. He dated the c,mmencement of the reform era from the building of Padgate schools. He did not take credit to himself for assisting in any way to build theism schools' but he bad the honour of sitting on the Board before the schools were occupied and had been a member of the School,' Committee ever since. He was a bit pre- judiced agalost the schools at the commencement and if he bad been a member of the Board at tne time when the griestion of building them was discussed, he would probably have voted against the txpenditure of such a large sum of money. but having worked on the schools committee for 18 years he could teatify to the excellent results ob- taiced. (Hear, hear.) Mr Jenner Fus.t's predecessor once told them that within a very short time they would have to build two more homes, hut from that date for- ward,the average was less and the reduction of paupers waa largely due to the children being brought up in these rchools. Great improvement had taken place in the coedition of the people in the workhouse and now they had an new ihfirmary with all its modern appoint- ments and conveniences. The proceedings then terminated. Light refreshments were subsequently partaken of and the health of Me (Pm mid Mr Liouglatga drunk.

WARRINGTON'S ANCIENT BUILDINGS. Visit by the Lancashire and Cheshire Historic Society

Some Interesting Facts. On Saturday afternoon, the members of the Historic Society ot Lancashire and Cheshire visited Warrington for the purpose of inspecting the Parish Church, Holy Trinity Church, the Museum, and the Old Academy. Among the local gentlemen who accompanied them were :—Alderman Shaw Green, qouncillor A. Bennett. Ur. C. Madeley, and 41r. T. May (a member of the society). The party was received at the Parish Church by the Rev. Canon Willis, who gave an int( resting his- ) tory of the building. The church, the visitors were informed, was dedicated to St. Elphin, celtic saint of the 7th century. The Saxon Church in 700 AD, Was of mud and wattle. From 1150 to 1200 a stone Norman Church stood, which was superseded in 1354 by a decora- ted stone church,of which now only the chancalremains. The founder was Sir William Boteler, aud a little later in the same century the Lady chapel was built sad the tombs of the founder and his wife placed in a recess in the north.ln 1463 Sir John Boteler and Margaret Gerard his wife died, and an alabaster tomb was placed in the chapel. In 1464 a slab was found in the Massey Chapel which WB9 supposed to mark the tomb to the Rector, Thomas Massey. Sir Thomas Boteler died in 1522,and his tomb was formerly a little west of that new in the chapel. " In 1696 the tower of the, church .was re-built, and in 1781 the nave underwent a similar change. The crypt vestry was discovered by Rector Rawstoun in 1824. to 1833 the present south aisle was built by Rector Powys, and during years between 1859 and 1869 the present tower and spire, the north and south chapels, the nave ani north aisle were re.built by Rector Queckett. Canon Willis accompanied the visitors through the church, and spoke interestingly of its old associations. The very fine tomb to Sir John Bete:et and his. wife excited the keen interest of the party, and Mr Beamoat's lucid description of it, as read by the Rector, had their appreciative attention. The Rector mentioned inciden- tally that he proposed to erect an altar and use the chapel, and as tbe tomb is below the fl or line, he asked for information upon the advisability of raising it He was generally advised to lower the floor of the chapel. After an inspection of the chancel, and an ex- amination of some old prints of the church, the party proceeded to what is, perhaps, the most interesting portion of the church, the crypt vestry, which was discovered under such romantic circumstances. Here they inspected the splendid silver plate and old docu- ments with profound interest the while they listened to descriptions from the Rector, Mr May, and Mr Lloyd, the vestry clerk. After a walk through the churchyard, the party pro- ceeded to the Rectory, where the Rector and Mee Willie hospitably entertained them. Trinity Church was next visited, and here Mr Madeley gave a brief description of , the builducg. The original church was built in 1709. The present edifice was erected in 1760. Tha visitors heard with interest that the massive candelabra in the centre of the church came from the old House of Com- mons wince was destroyed by fire in the reign of George 11. The tower of the church was given by an old time , governor of the town, and since its erection the curfew bell has been rung at eight each evening. Tbe painting above the centre of the altar is a copy from the Louvre by a local artist. After a short stay in the church the party went on to the Museum and under Mr Madeley's guidance spent an entertaining and instructive hour inspecting the well-arranged and coenprehension collection of natural and scientific curiosities and works of art. An object of particular interes: shown to the visitors w ehesuble found built up in a disused t Parish Church in 1824. It afterwards p the property of Sc Alban's Church,Bewsey-street. It was Saeig passed h as e ieralt alOt eit the the hands hands of the Catholic clergy, and is now kindly lent for the occasion by the Rector, the Rev Father Whittle. From the Museum the party went to the Old Academy, where they had tea. They also in- spected the building, and most of them having a know- ledge of the intellectual associations of the place, found a deep interest in what they saw and heard. The party afterwards returned to the Museum, and ass( mbled in the lecture room under the presidency of Mr J Paul Rvlands, F.S.A., to hear a lecturo by Mr , Madeley on " Early Warrington printed books." In ad- dition to a number of broadsides, prints and drawings relation to the, Parish Church, there was laid out for the visitors' inspection a collection of books, chiefly the production of the well-known Byres Press, and the rarer books from the Library. Some specimens of very fine printing were greatly admired. The leeture also was highly appreciated. At the close of the proceedings cordial votes of thanks were passed to Mr Madeley and to the Rector (Canon Willis). THE BUDGET.

War Expenditure.

In Parlivry-nt y•aterday the Ch%nrellor of the Exchequer Rai 1 hit the total exoenclirure laet year was £183,592,000, of which over £63,000,000 went to the war. _ _ .

Sir M Pscimxted he deficiency of this year at £53,347 000.

Budget at a Glance.

The Chancellor proposes to rnret the deficiency by the following proposals : Twopence on the income-tax, making is 23 alto- gether. A duty oa sugar. On refined sugar 4421 per cwt ; on raw sugar dimini:hing in certain proportion to 2s per ewt. Fixed duty on molasses of 2s per cwt. A duty of is 8d per cwt on glucose. The proposal with regard to glucose was put for- ward tentatively, and he was prepared to hear rep- resentations on the subject. He estimated that the sugar duty would yield £5,100,000 this year, after allowing for three weeks that had gone. No increase on wine, or tea, or tcbacco. Export duty of is a ton on coal. The duty on coal is expected to produce £2,100,000.

Shipowners by devoting more bunker space to coal, and less to cargo, could escape the duty. The increasson income tax and the duties on sugar and coal would yield altogether eleven millions. He again proposed to suspend the Sinking Fund, and there, therefore, would be the deficit of 39 millions.

It was proposed to borrow 60 millions. Arrangements were made that if the Transvaal prospered in the future, ,bat country should bear a portion of the cost of the war. Be seeka power to borrow on Console. Death of Lady Newton.

The Dowager Lady Newton died on Wednesday evening at 20, Belgra ye Sq Aare, London, of pneu- monia. The deceased lady was the daughter of the yen. Charles Wodehouse, irchdeacon of Norwich, and married in 1356 the first Baron Newton, who died in 1893. Lady Newtan had a residence at Lyme Park, Stockport. An Election Libel.

A Liberal Candidate Obtains £3OO Damages against Messrs. M'Corquodale and Co. In the King's Bench Divson, ,esterday, the hear- ing was resumed of the action for damages brought by Mr. J. VV. Bean, L.C.C., against M'Corquodale and Company, printers. The libel was contained in a pamphlet. the name .4f the writer of which could not be ascertained. The libel, which was admitted, was that Mr Bann was in favour of fat pensions to wealthy people, that at the St. George's election petition proceedings he was disqualified from ever sitting in Parliament, that he was a representative of social disorder, a fact which was app.rent from the class of people who gave evidence on his side during the petition, and that his evidence on that occasion was all moonshine, The pamphlet was circulated just before the polling day at Bermond- sey during the last election, where Mr Bean was a candidate. H. was defeated by three hundred

votes. The jury returnel a yr rd.c: for plaintiff, damages £3OO. Judgment accordingly.

A Runcorn Man's Opinion of the C.l.V.'s.—lnteresting Facts.

Lance-Corpl. Caulfield, of the let Royal Monster Fusiliers, General Paget's Brieade, writes from Brook- horst Soruit. Transvaal. to his parents at Runcorn, under date March 14th, 1991. " I am quite sick of fhia war, and goodness knows how soon it will be over. I auPpose the public thinks the war is over now the C,l.V.'s. are at biome. Its enough to make the ordinary Tommy cry to read the papers cracking the C.l.V.'s. up. I. as well as my comrade 4, would like to know if there have not been any other volunteers wbo have fought for their country, and given up homes and situa- tione, besides the C.I.V 's. I ask in the name et my comrades, "what have the Lord Mayor'S Own done out here, more than any other volunteer? " The 'Graphic' Publiahed a Onto of the C.l.V.'a. showing all their backs with the reading. "What the Boers never saw." We would very much like to know haw they retired, when they got the command to do so? Did they walk backwards ? I certainly do not think much of our people at home, who created such a atir ever the C.I.V-'s. return. If they have done their work better than any other volunteers, why have they been sent home first, especially SR the country is in such an unsettled state ? By the papers. they have the credit of finishing the war. If so ashy are the rern.inder of us left out here? Volun- teers and regulars are fighting every day. As regards the C I.V.'a roughing it. they never knew what it was to be hungry, thev never had eight rm. of " Flout ' served out to them for a dap's rationcafter coming of a hialg and weary mareh;"hey never slept out on the open veldt without a hisnket or a coat to cover them when it I was fret Zing hard. But Paget'a volunteers did. The C.l.V.'s

never had to manta without trousers er boo's. I saw a volunteer with only an old potato sack tied around him and another poor fellow with pieceti of raw bide, tied with ening to keep his feet I rom the hard rough ground Just a few concluding questions. I believe a C.I.V. has been recommended for the Victoria Cross, for filling water bottles under are. Now I wonder does it ever strike the peopl at home that the Si me thing had to be done by ourselves scores of tin:mi, but we had to do it without being chierved, or we should have got a court martial for leaving the ranks. At Rhenoster Kop,a few ' weeks ago, there was only a small pool f water. The Boers concentrated their fire on this pool ; yet men went with their lyirtles for wat.r, which had to be got for ;he vioundtd. Yet none of thorn got the V.C. Let honour be given here honour is due, and there will be less bitter feeling amongst all ranks.

Pensions for Wounded Soldiers. At the first general meeting of the Soldiers' and Sailors' Help Society (Snath West Lancashire branch), held under the presidency of Earl Dc•rtv at Liverpool on Friday, the bon secretary, Captain Hughes, vx- plained that the obje, t of the society was to help invalided soldiers and sailors who bad fought for their , , King and country, by money grants and employment. South-a eit Laneanhire had to a grelt extent been , mapped out, and a number of gentlemen had undertaken ,1 to look after these, and see that respectable and time- r expired soldiers and railors did not drift into the work— hou.se, but were found work if they could do it, or help them with funds Thus, the Earl of Lathom bad under- taken to lock after the Ormskirk district, Dr Bates would look after St Helens, Colonel Filzherbert attended to Warringtoe, and Canon Mitchell to Prescot, and there were a r umber of helpers for smaller districts. The Earl of Derby warned the society against overlapping, and said be believed the Government intended be pen- sion all the soldiers wounded in the war, though he did not think that applied to invalided men as well. A com- mittee was appointed o cover the work in Liverpool ; the rest of the business was left over until the society has been organized.

Sir Gilbert, was not, he was told, able to be present on that occasion. rhat was a source of regret, but be hoped they would be able to educate him better as begot older. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) Without detaining them too much he Should like to say a few words on another matter. When he was a boy at school he was taught that the population of Warrington was 15,000. He was expecting the remits of the present census to be some- thing like 70,000. He had had a talk with Mr Terbuck, and they had been considering what responsibilities lay upon Warrington in connection with the Church of England and education generally. He believed it would not be very long before there would be one universal education rate for the whole of the realm. Its Germany that was the case. The religious difficulty was managed ia some way there, and surely they in England could manage it also. The cost of education should be raised by a fund, and the religious part of the question, which was said to be difficult, could be left to their management as a corn- monsenee people. He believed they would manage it very well. If the money could be provided the other riart would be well managed. He was sorry to learn from a Manchester paper that morning that they had many uneducated people even in Warrington. From the annual report of the Registrar-General of &VW, Deaths aed Marriages in England in 1699, he found that there was a little reflection upon Warrington. It said that among the persons who married, 50 per 1,000 of the husbands and 165 per 1,000 of the wives were miome— that was under 21 years of age. Improvident marriages no doubt accounted for a great deal of the distress which existed. Among the districts which showed a large I Proportion of illiterate brides and bridegrooms were Dudley, Wigan, Warrington, and Manchester. War- rington was thus one of the four mentioned, and he hoped all present would in their private capacity assist those who were working so hard to tare that reproach from them. (Hear, hear.) They did not want at Ail events to appear to have so many illiterates. He begged to propose a vote of thanks to Lady Greenall for bar services on that oecaidon. (Applause.) Alderman SUTTON seconded the vote of thanks. With Mr Tarim* bethought they were very fortunate in having secured the services of Lady Greenall to open their sale of work. He was sorry her husband was not able to be present, because it would have afforded him (the speaker) the pleasure of telling him most decisively how fortunate he was and bow thankful he ouPht to be in having obtained such an excellent wife. (Hear, hear.) De was sure that if Sir Gilbert would follow her example and be guided by her, the less of the late Sir Gilbert Greenall would not be An keenly felt as it was at the present time. He hoped Sir Gilbert and his wife would be spared to live amongst them for many years. (Ap- plause.) The motion was heartily agreed to. Mr Taylor was tli r •fore elected and took the chair.—Mr Roebuck was elected vice- chairman, on the proposition of Admiral Jones Parry, seconded by Mr Hatton.—The overseers, Messrs Astall, and Houghton, were unanimously re elected. The Clerk said he had received a letter from the engineer of the Manchester chip Canal Company to the effect that he had laid the Clerk's letter before the Board of Directors and that they had decided to erect a small shelter on the northerly side of the canal. At a later stage of the meeting Mr. Roebuck moved that the Clerk write to the Ship Canal Cvnpany thanking them for erecting the shelter. Mr. Warburtm seconded, and it was agreed to. The Clerk said that he and the Chairman attended a demonstration in connec ion with the new steam fire engine at Lymm. Mr Rose, the engineer, suggested that all the Parish Councils wtar were con- tributing £5 a year for the use of the engine should erect in a suitable position in the parish two lengths of fire hose with branch pipes. Mr Rose sent in an estimate ' which included two lengths of hose 25 yards long, with all ancessories, branch pipes be., and a suitable box fnr them to be kept in, for the sum of Sat Eventually it was decided that the matter lie over until the next meet- ing. Mr. Roebuck Inquired why the reports and the correspondence relating to the" nuisance S' in the dis- trict were headed "The Thelwall Nuisance." Mr Long's tanaery was in the township of Grappenha 11, the brook in which the effluent ibwed was in Grappenhall, and yet was called " The Thelwall Nuisance." Admiral Jones-Parry said he had tried to get the phrase corrected, the reporters called it the " Tnelwall ' nuisance. Mr Astall said he would like it to go forth that the nuisance did not exist in Thelwall.• Admiral Junes-Parry said the effluent was now ap- proved as fit to go into the canal.

Great Sankey. A meetinz of the Great Sankey Parish Council was held in the Bpard Sehool,Great Sankey,on Tuesday even- ing,the chair being taken pro tern by Mr Thomas Halton. Mere were also present : —Messrs William Taylor, Joseph H Kilshaw, Gorge Thompson, J Fulton Smith, Henry Singleton and Joseph Smith (clerk):

===== 0002601_19010419_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTION OF CHAIRMAN.

Mr Halton proposed Mr George Thompson as Chair- man of the Council for the ensiling year, and expressed the hope that the election would be a unanimous one. In Mr Thompson they had a emitleman who took a great interest in the district. He had worked with him on the parish Council for two or three years previous to the last Council, and whilst they differed OA many occa- sions and there was a little faiction, they made headway. Without friction they could not make headway. He hoped they would be more progressive than they had been during the last three years. They had, however, made good strides in the district in a quiet way. They had not been an expensive Council and every member had had the interests of the township at heart. (Hear, bear ) Mr. Fulton Smith seconded and Mr gilshaw supported the motion, which was put to the meeting and carried unanimously. Mr; Thompson, on taking the chair, said he was very much obliged to the members for the flattering terms which had been made use of with regard to him. He was not new to this kind of work. He waz elected a member of the Pariah Council at the first election that ! took place in that township, and he thought he could ' say without fear of contradiction that as far as he was able he did the bes; he could for the township and every person in it. With regard to friction,he would not give much for any Council which got along without it. He had worked on many bodies, but he had never known a ! Council worth anything where there had been no friction which stimulated people to do more work than they would otherwise have done. Some of the farmers of the district appeared to be under tha impression that he was inclined to be extravagant, but that was a very grave mistake. Fie, above everyone else should be very cautious as to the spending of money unneoessarily. The company with which he was connected were large rate- ! ! payers in the district, and it was to his interest to keep ' the rates down as much as he could, but it was not right, it was not just to people who built property, for the Council to use false economy. If they were called upon to spend money they must do it to the best of their ability. (Hear. hear.)

THE MEETINGS OF THE COUNCIL.

A long discussion took place with reference to the selection of the dates for tha meetings of the Council for the ensuing yeor, Mr Singleton proposing that the meet- ings be held on a Tuesday night in every month. Mr litishaw seconded. Mr Halton moved that six meetings be fixed, namely, on the same night all tile meetings of the School Board, but on alternate months. 1 Mt Singleton seconded. The Clerk thought that it would not be wise to appoint monthly meetings for several reasons, which he named. Other meetings would be held when necessary. The resolution was carried. POSTLL MASTERS.

INCREASED FACILITIES REQUIRED. ' Mr Halton introduced the question of the need of additional postal facilities for the district. He explained that some time age a similar matter was brought for- ward and come good was done, but they required a further improvement, namely, a delivery of letters on it Sunday. Sankey was a growing district, and it was a great hardship that their letters should be at the Post Office from Saturday to Monday. The Poet Office in the village had been a great acquisi- tion. The arrangements were good except with I regard to Sunday delivery and the question of telegrams. No harm would be done by asking the Post- master of Warrington to consider the matter. Mr Singleton remarked that there was a Sunday I delivery at his end of the township, but there was no collection on a Sunday night. On that night their letters had to be sent to Penketh. They needed a collection as well as a delivery. The Clerk : There is a collection at Sankey Bridges. Mr Singleton : But there ought to be one at the Post Office at Great Sankey on a Sunday night. Mr Halters was willing for that matter also to be brought under the notice of the Postmaster. Mr Singleton : Ask for plenty and you wil get some- thing. Mr Halton expressed the opinion that the Warrington Postmaster had done the best he could to promote the interests of the people living in the district of Great Sankey. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman thought it would be better for a depu- tation to wait upon him, and the suggestion met with the approval of the meeting, Messrs Fulton Smith, Halton and the Clerk being appointed. THE LIGHTING OF THE TOWNSHIP. Mr Singleton called attention to the lighting of the distric*,ani pointed out that the lamps were turned out before twelve o'clock. .

It was decided that thn Clerk should mako enquiries and report to the next meeting.

SORE THROAT.

I If you suffer from a sore and congested throat, if you cannot swallo-er without pain, if your voice is hard, and if every now and again you have to cough in order to clear the passage from the lungs, it is quite evident you are in a very serious state of ill-health, and should exer- cise the greatest care, and, if possible, stay indoors and in one warm atmosphere until you are better again. Important Order. On Wednesday it was ascertained that the executive of the five Cheshire Volunteer battalions have decided upon an important alteration in the annual encamp- ment. It has been arranged that battalions forming the brigade shall go into camp at Conway from August 11th to 18th, instead of at Whitsuntide, as previously. No doubt the change will be welcomed, as cold nights and mornings should ha exchanged for congenial summer weather. The only drawback is that Whitsuntide boll. dar s have been con V enient ; but, doubtless, after the recent patriotic respo rises, employers of labour will rise to the occasion. Last year the Cheshires encamped on Salisbury Plain.

Tat CRAIRMLNSHIP.

Mr W Handley said it was his duty, privilege, and pleasure to propose that Mr R a Posnett be the chair- man of the Council for the year they were just entering. He did so without the slightest shadow of disappoint- ment—his name having been before the private meeting which took place prior to the Council—for he was in great sympathy with the general expression that the Chairmanship should go tbe round, and be held for one year only. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Lightburn espres,+ed great pleasure in seconding, feeling quite aura that Mr. Poeuett would snow in the future what he had shown in the p st,—the qualities of a very good member. (fies.r• hoar). The resolution was unanimously carried. Mr. Handley. as retiring chairman then transferred the chain of office from his own person to that of Mr. Pormett, remarking 13,m tieised he was to do so and at the cams time to introduce Mr Posnett to the leng and honourable succession. (Hear, hear.) fie felt mute Mr Posnett would have the !ull support of the Council, and that be would fill the chair with dignity to himself, and with honour to all of them. (Hear, hear.) Mr Posnett, on taking the chair, said : Mr Handley and gentlemen, I can assure you that when I came here three-quarters of au hour ago, I did uot think that the honour of presiding would now be in my hands, and I really den'; know what to say under the circumstances. I have been in Runcorn about 13 years. I cline as a lad and I was then only earning a bare pittanee, just enough to keep rue, but [rim that time forward every association I have had with the town has b...en to my advantage, and my 13 years in Rene orn have been an untold blessing to myself; (Hear, hear). I take the opportunity of t xpressing my et ep gratitude to all my friends in the town who have helped 1119 and shown me kindneee and that kindness has t last put me in this position. I desire during the year that ita before us to serve the town in the best way I can (Hear,l hear). During the last 13, years [think the town has I gone through a great many vissicitudes and a great many difficulties, but I think eerhaps, now, things are better than they were 3 or 4 years ago, and I hope they will go on improving. (Hoar, hear). There is ime thing further—one thing that occurs to me as I stand here, and that is this, I would like to see during the coming year some memorial erected in this town to Her late fiLlesty Queen Victoria. (Hear, hear.) Ido not know how it is to be done, but it does seem to me that tbe memorial to the Queen could be brought about if the inhabitants of the town determined to push for. ward the Cottage Hospital. It would also be an untold blessing to the working classes of the town. (Applause) If that goes forward I will do my best with work and money and everything that will help it forward in every possible way. (Hear, hear.) I thank you very much gentlemen for the honour you have done me and can only say how little I feel I have deserved it. Mr Posnett then prop )esed that the Council accord their sincere thanks an Mr Handley for his conduct in the chair during the year just past. He was sure that every member sitdog around the table felt that in Mr Handley they had had a chairman who was well up in ever) thing. (Hear, hear.) Be was a gentlenn n who bad served the Coun- cil with unfailing gallantry, dignity and attention, and he felt he was expressing all their wishes In giving utterance to the assurance that they accorded him their heartiest thanks for his services in the chair during the year past. (Hear, hear,) Mr Pritchard seconded with infinite pleasure. Mr Handley had discharged his duties in a gallant and dignified manner. (Hear, hear) He thought, indeed, that Mr Handley had alrnost served the town in every public capacity, and discharged his duties during his year of office with dignity and credit to he town. (hear. bear)—and be felt sure they would remember at some future time the faithfulness and diligence with which he performed his duties: (Hear, bear.) Mr S Taylor supported saying he did not think they had ever had a chairman who had given so much eaten- , [ion to the work as had Mr Handley. Speaking for himself he felt he could not have carried out as success- fully the work so ably accomplished by Mr Handley, who had given a great deal of his time and taken a great deal of trouble in every detail connected with the , work of the Council. He had much pleasure in support- ing. (Hear. hear ) Mr J. W. Lightburn did not want to "paint the lily," but quite endorsed all that had been said. Mr Handley had certainly spent a great deal of time, and made him • self almost indispensible. Indeed he felt that if Mr Handley bad been paid a very large salary he could not have served the council better. The vote of thanks woe most unanimously accorded. Mr Handley said he was very much obliged for the kindly feeling r xpressed. He felt be had but served them imperfectly at times, but was gratified to have met with their approval. That vote of thanks was sufficient reward for anything he might have done, and the kind words of Mr Posnett, Mr Pritchard, Mr Payln: and Mr Lightburn he would not very readily forget. He also expressed his gratitude to the mem- bers who had voted so unanimously. In the future RS in the past he would do his utmost for Runcorn in what- ever capacity he could. (Hear, hear.)

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

[By " Ovalist.'')

Ia the final at last! Warrington male no mistake about defeating Castleford on Saturday,and have there- fore qualified to appear at Ileadingley on Saturday next, the 27th inst., to decide whether he Northern Union Cup shall rest at Warrington or Batley for the ensuing twelve months. The Batley youths did a great thing in lowering the pride of Spindledona, but there is any amount of confi lance hereabmta, that they will meet their match in Warrington. Speculations of this nature however, I will leava till next week.

No doubt there was much " cussing " on Saturday when the-morning broke, not flue and clear, but dull and wet. Rain fell persistently and the spirits of the Warrington folk also fell irt proportionate ratio. But soon after noon the clowls drifted away and " o.d Sal " c4rne out to in, ke amends as fast as he culd. and the same viriis were immediately soaring sky-high in joy- ful cxoectancy. Si much for the effect of the weather on the pople. On the Wheater's Field it bad a more lasting effect, the" turf" being a sodden m4as of mud which merited the sneer of a Warringtoo enthusiast who remarked " Ca' this a field ? why it's nowt but a river with'tide run eaow." Anyhow it was better than the stuff which did duty for a field at Rochdale a year ago and though the referee was the same, the result was Erikingly different.

The Warringtm men were full of confidence and other good things. When I dropped into the Cale for my "little snack "on Saturday morning I found the team all doing manful duty round the hospitable board, and siding honest English beef with a celerity which aston- ished even the waitresses,than whose calm dignity there is nothing more impresslve—in the Cale. Tne faces of the players were wreathed Ha smiles the mot expan- sive, and there is no questioning the wisdom of the policy which to, k them to the Cafe for the cqinaf rting of in ward qualms. They all looked and felt fit to fight for a kingdom after the operation, and when I caught them up once more at Broughton there was the same cheerful confidence apparent. In the dressing-roorn they were arguing thr point as tol who should open the nor- ing, and as Fish has hittr rto att raded to this matter ' himself, the forwards decided unanimously that they would open the ball themselves. And they kept their promise tor Eien got the first try which laid the founda- tion for a big score.

Though the morning, was sl unproadiging, the Warrington spectators journeyed to Broughton in large numbers, and whichever way you turned there WOle to be seen primrose and blue fa v,urs in either cdp or coat. As primroses and violets are just in se Aim, these prvtty flowers were in large demand, and ti) crowd lo•ked more like members of a horicultural society than foot- ball enthusiasts.

Mancheatn. was enveloped in sunshine when we got there, and the crowd reflect up in large numbers as 330 approached, though the morning's rain undoubt- edly kedt a large number away who would otherwise have witnessed the battle for suprc- m+cy an(' place in the final tie. Sltne tight or nine th msand people assombled and the proceeds were £224 —curiouily enough just Z.I. more than was taken at Warrington in the Bradford match. This is rather did- appointing, but the financial standpoint is not the principal one in Warrington eyes just at present.

---- Isherword won th toss, and showed good general- ship by electing to play against tho wind in the first half. It was certain that Castleford would make their effort while the elements favoured them, and the Wdr- rington kipper probably relied upon his defence to keep the sheet clean up to the interval, and upon the speed of his men to get points in the second half. His course of action was justified by results for Castleford ran them- selves to a standstill by half-time and made not the ghost of a show afterwards.

Things did not look particularly hAlialthy for the first quarter ot an hour, as Casileford were the first to accom- rnodate themselves to the sticky ground, and drove War- iington back by sheer strength and long kicking. The Warrington defence seemed decidedly rocky and it was not long before Hallam foolishly kicked out when he might have let the hall go dead, and Smith kicked a goal from a mark. This was just the spur Warrington needed. Aided by clever play from Fish and Isherwood they raced to Cho other elid and E len charging dawn Burns' kick on the line, got a soft try behind the poste which Fish easily improved. The Lancashire men's shout had hardly died away when a misunderstandin g between Hallam and Dickenson gave the Castleford for- wards an easy try at the other end, and the game was five all." Warrington had got their footing now, however, and a swinging pining bout baween Bate. Isherwood, and Fish ended in the latter scoring near the posts and kicking a second goal. At half time Warring- ton L d by 10 puinte to 5 points.

The secaid half needs litt!e describing. Castleford were soon beaten to the world and hopelessly outclassed forward and beck. Warrington got possession smartly and the backs simply waltzed r,.und the defence, Fish getting in at the corner again, after beautiful combina- tion. Another try came Irom Dickenson, who aid one of his unexpected swerves when everybody was mark- ing Barris and grounded near the stumps. It was a pretty effort, but Fish failed to -convert. Warrington eased up a bit, seemingly tired of running and paseing with scarcely any opposition. Castleford got to the other end, which was a misfortune for them, as they had to chase Fish all the way back again and see him deposit the oval behind the goal poets again. This was:the result of fine play by Isherwood and Dickenson who quite outwitted the .pposing centres and gave Fish a clear field. The nearest opponent was somewhere about the quarter flag when Fish scored, but he had a good view, and you know, Castleford intended to watch Fish. Isherwood raised the total to 21 points with a goal, and then Warrington rested on their oars till the final solo sounded,when the Tykes were soundly beaten and a wee bit despondent in the bargain.

flie mystery is, how Castleford have reached the semi-final. A team with less idea of what Rugby foot- ball really is I never saw. They had absolutely no idea of combination, and when once, after three-quarter time,one of the halves passed the ball to a three•quarter, there was a loud ironical cheer. The methods of the two teams were in direct contrast, and nDthiog app t ared easier than to get tries by the quick passing game adop- ted by Warrington. CaAleford had no more idea < f coping with it than they had of copying it. Even for- ward they held no advantage after the first quarter of an hour, the Warrington eight completely outplaying their heavier opponents as time went on. But for the efforts of one man, Smith, the Yorkshire team must have sustained a fearful drubbing. This player did yeoman service in the centre, and his fielding and punt- ing were really first class all through the game. Ile seems wasted in a team like Castleford.

Certainly the best man on the Bill writhe Warrington captain Dui Isherwood, who played a really superb game throughout, He made all the running for Fiats, who was in irresistible form, and at the same time kept in touch with Dickenson and Hsrris on the right. Both these did well, but the latter suffer( d from a tendency to selfistinsss on the part of the younger player. Dick- enson ought not to neglect his wing man even for the pleasure of scoring such a fins try as he did. Bate was a bit slow at timss and would do well to sling the ball more promptly when he has obtained possession of it. Hallam was not quite up to the mark, bat improved as the game grew older. He will have to be imarter,thougb to cope with Batley.

Dedication of the New Organ.

Sermon by the Bishop of Chester. St Michael's Church, Runcorn, was on Wednesday' evening packed to over flowing, the occasion being the opening and dedication of the new organ. The RigtO Rev De Jayne, Bishop of Coester, was present and preached. The other clergy taking part in the service were:—Rev Canon A M Wood (vicar of Runcorn), Rev 0 H Killick (vicar of Holy Trinity), &Avgw 13 Stables, B Firth, W Scholes, C P Bashforth, J N Poole, S Special hymna were sung, psalms chanted, and the aur mented choirs of Runcorn Parish Chu ch and St Michael 1 Church very effectiv.ly rendered the anthem " 0 praise God in His. Holiness."

The Right Reverend Bishop Jayne, preached all eloquent sermon from the text " la the day of fY power shall the people offer Thee freewill offerings with holy worship," Psalm cr., part of 3rd verse. In the first place, be said, did they use and realise the power of our Lord ? Was that power a reality in their lives, in their prayers, in their work, and did they derell strength for their weakness by His Ahnighty Power r .'When lam weak, then and then only am I strong.l Were they working on the side of that power or were they working against it ? It was a most solerne question, and it was a question they should take heed of 'bet:rues. "And in the day of Thy Power" they rea' On "ball the people offer thee free-will offerings with a holy worship." Free-will offerings—" not grudgingly' or of necessity for Gad loveth a cheerful giver." Such 'rat the spirit in which the gift was to be conveyed, and he did not doubt that the offering they offered that night was a free-will offering, and was one of as multitude of similar offerings illustrative 0' the spirit which was abroad in the Chute' of Christ. That spirit which made men, worm°, and children. The War Bkhop thsn dealt with the aspect of worship which should be holy ; and spoke of the power of music, architecture,and the decorative art" in the church. They were not, however, to allow theae things to become the whole of their worship, but were, to worship with the understaadiag of tidy, mind, An'. soul. Without that their worship areuid become a' I worthy and degenerate into fanaticism. Finally he prayed that the beautiful instrument they had Ps', dedicated to the service of G..d arculd never be t2O. worthily used. sj'At tae conclusion of the service, Mr T J Hugbet assistant organist of Chester Cathedral, gave an urge° recital, which was listened to with marked attentions and which fully disp'ayed.the powers and scope of the instrument. The following were the icerns:_Toecata and Fugue in D minor, Bach ; Allegretto, Wider Scherzo, Hoffmann ; Air (with variations), Hayde Concerto in F, Elandel; Romance,Wheeldon ; Priere arl° Berceuse, Guilmant; Bridal Music, Wagner. We amend the following particulars :—The organ was built by Messrs P Conacher and Cempany,Huddera; field, to designs and specifications drawn up by Mr T Hughe.,assiktint organist of Chester Cathelral who als° supervised its erection. The case is of pitcb-pinesand harmony with the rest rf the church fAtures and sea`; ing. The organ is three manual, and will ba blown Dr hydraulic power. The swell organ comprises eleven stops, and thegreat organ 7 stops ; choir organ 5 star; pedal organ 2 stops. There are six ccuplers over °to keyboard ; choir to pedals; great to pedals ; swell tl pedals ; 'swell to great Swell to choir; swell - suboctave ; and s'ar composition pedals.
